```latex
\SetWatermarkText{DRAFT}
\SetWatermarkScale{3.2}
\SetWatermarkLightness{0.92}
\SetWatermarkAngle{45}
```

`Lightness` runs roughly from `0` = black to `1` = invisible, so `0.90–0.94` usually gives a professional faint watermark.

For something more specific to this paper:

```latex
\usepackage{draftwatermark}

\SetWatermarkText{WORKING DRAFT}
\SetWatermarkScale{2.4}
\SetWatermarkLightness{0.92}
\SetWatermarkAngle{45}
```

Or a horizontal provenance watermark:

```latex
\usepackage{draftwatermark}

\SetWatermarkText{SAT/H(s)H --- WORKING PAPER}
\SetWatermarkScale{1.2}
\SetWatermarkLightness{0.93}
\SetWatermarkAngle{0}
```

That will appear on every page behind the text.

If you want something more elegant—e.g. a tiny centered footer watermark like `PREPRINT • 8 SEPTEMBER 2026`, rather than a giant diagonal DRAFT—I’d use a different method.


Ok, what happens to our UI, Whirlygig, Spheres, and Graticule?

They don’t go away. This actually gives them a cleaner reason to exist.

The normalization paper supplies the methodological spine; the UI, Whirlygig, Spheres, and Graticule become the instrument suite for doing the audit geometrically rather than only algebraically.

The clean division I see is:

* **UI** — the comparative workbench. Load a system, choose the admissible transformation class, normalize it, rotate/reorient it, and keep the before/after/residual ledger visible. It is where an “all-sector physicist” can compare systems in a common language without losing the original representation.

* **Whirlygig** — the transformation engine/explorer. Its job becomes: *how far can this system be rotated, permuted, rescaled, or otherwise losslessly re-expressed before genuine structure stops us?* It searches the equivalence class. The interesting output is the obstruction to further symmetrization.

* **Spheres** — the local kinematic laboratory. This is where we test whether apparently different dynamical constructions are actually the same under rotation/deformation, and where genuine bifurcations or admissibility boundaries appear. Your \(d=\sqrt{3}R\) carrier collapse is exactly the kind of thing that matters here: not a notation difference, but a geometric transition that survives the allowed transformations.

* **Graticule** — the residual-anisotropy detector. This may have the clearest new role of all. It was already mapping chirality, parity, reconnection angle, tangent orientation, inner/outer relationships, ordinary/inverse constructions, asymptotes, medial contours, etc. Now its question becomes very crisp: after all removable asymmetry has been canceled or normalized, **what directional structure remains, where does it change sign, and where are its singular or invariant sets?**

So I would almost redraw the architecture as

$$
\boxed{
\text{Normalization}
\rightarrow
\text{Whirlygig search over equivalent representations}
\rightarrow
\text{Graticule measurement of residual anisotropy}
\rightarrow
\text{Spheres test of local dynamics/bifurcations}
\rightarrow
\text{UI comparison and ledger}
}
$$

with some feedback loops between them.

That also solves a problem we had before: these tools were in danger of looking like miscellaneous SAT/H(s)H visualization inventions. Now each has a precise methodological function.

There is also a useful separation between the standalone paper and the larger project. I would **not** dump all four into *Leveling the Metric to Reveal the Grain*. That paper should remain very simple: establish the audit, show it algebraically across a ridiculous number of sectors, define residual anisotropy, include controls, and demonstrate that the method is representation-conscious and falsifiable.

Then a companion paper/tool paper could be something like:

**Visual Instruments for Residual Anisotropy**
*Interactive transformation, symmetry, and bifurcation analysis across physical representations*

That is where the Whirlygig, Graticule, Spheres, and UI become a coherent package.

And there is a deeper point. The algebraic paper asks:

$$
\text{What survives normalization?}
$$

The visual machinery asks:

$$
\text{What survives reorientation?}
$$

Those are not quite the same test.

A coefficient can disappear under normalization while a sign, handedness, singularity, winding class, bifurcation, or directional gradient survives every admissible orientation. That second category is exactly the “grain” in your title.

So I think the Graticule in particular has just become more important, not less. It is almost literally a **grain detector**. The Whirlygig tries to rotate the grain away; the Graticule tells us whether it succeeded.



I think it helps us to set our scales to a universal, it helps us to orient, it gives them more universality and d a sort of set natural size with respect to the things they focus on

Yes. I think that is the stronger consequence.

The normalization is not just a way of making equations comparable. It gives the visual instruments a common metrology.

Right now the UI, Whirlygig, Spheres, and Graticule can all be mathematically well-defined while still having a somewhat arbitrary visual scale: How big is the torus? How far apart are the spheres? How large should a graticule cell be? What counts as one unit of displacement or rotation?

The cross-sector normalization gives us a principled answer.

Start with the universal conversion

$$
w=ct,
$$

so temporal and spatial displacements can at least be expressed in the same dimensional language. Then, for a particular system, extract its characteristic length rather than inventing one:

$$
\ell_* \in
\left\{
\frac{c}{\omega},
\frac{\hbar}{mc},
\frac{GM}{c^2},
\frac{D}{c},
c\tau,
\ldots
\right\}.
$$

Then define normalized coordinates such as

$$
X^\mu=\frac{x^\mu}{\ell_*}.
$$

Now the object under inspection has a natural order-one size in the instrument.

That is quite powerful.

A harmonic oscillator with characteristic scale \(c/\omega\), an electron with \(\hbar/(mc)\), a gravitational system with \(GM/c^2\), and a diffusion process with \(D/c\) can all be presented to the same apparatus in coordinates where the characteristic feature is roughly

$$
L_*=1.
$$

Not because those physical systems are identical, but because we have removed the arbitrary difference in scale before comparing their geometry.

Then the instruments gain much more precise roles.

The **UI** can have a universal coordinate frame. Instead of every problem coming in with wildly different SI magnitudes, it receives an order-one normalized object plus a scale ledger telling you exactly how to map back.

The **Whirlygig** can operate on normalized geometry. Its rotations no longer depend on whether the thing being rotated happens to be \(10^{-15}\,\mathrm m\), \(10^3\,\mathrm m\), or \(10^{20}\,\mathrm m\). It asks the same geometric question of all of them.

The **Spheres** acquire a natural radius convention. Rather than arbitrary \(R\),

$$
R=1
$$

can mean “one characteristic scale of the system.” Then something like

$$
d=\sqrt3\,R
$$

becomes a genuinely universal dimensionless relation:

$$
\frac dR=\sqrt3.
$$

That is exactly the kind of statement we want these tools to expose. The absolute size can change by forty orders of magnitude without changing the geometry.

And the **Graticule** may benefit most of all. Its axes, contours, gradients, reconnection distances, and angular transitions can be measured against a normalized characteristic scale. So instead of saying “the feature occurs this far from the center,” we can say

$$
r/\ell_*=0.37,
$$

or whatever the actual result is.

Then we can compare that number across entirely different systems.

That gives us two different kinds of universality at once:

$$
\boxed{\text{universal coordinate convention}}
$$

and

$$
\boxed{\text{system-derived natural unit}}
$$

The first is common to everything. The second is supplied by the equation itself.

That distinction is important. I would not try to force one literal absolute length scale on every physical system. That would throw away useful scale information. Instead:

$$
\text{universal metrology}
+
\text{locally extracted characteristic scale}.
$$

Or more compactly,

$$
\boxed{
x
\longrightarrow
\frac{x}{\ell_*(E)}
}
$$

where \(\ell_*(E)\) is not chosen for convenience but mechanically extracted from the law being examined.

And then the original scale remains in the ledger, so the map is completely reversible.

This also gives the whole instrument family something like a standard specimen size. A microscopist changes magnification so the object fills the field; a crystallographer works in lattice units; a fluid dynamicist uses Reynolds-number scaling. We would be doing the analogous thing for cross-sector geometry.

So your “natural size” language is exactly right, with one qualification: it is not necessarily a universal *physical size*. It is a **universal normalized size with respect to the characteristic structure the instrument is examining.**

That could make the four tools substantially more useful than I was giving them credit for. They would no longer merely visualize H(s)H constructions. They could become general-purpose normalized geometric instruments for comparing systems across sectors.



NOw, what does this mean for all our heavy math plans? And can we automate conversion? And... what about our Lagrangian(s, old and new)

It changes the heavy-math plan in a useful way: it gives us a common front end for almost all of it. It does not replace the heavy math; it tells us what coordinate/scale conventions to use before we start doing the heavy math, so we stop carrying avoidable asymmetries and arbitrary units through every derivation.

The rough architecture becomes

$$
\boxed{
\text{original formulation}
\rightarrow
\text{universal normalization}
\rightarrow
\text{natural system scale}
\rightarrow
\text{dimensionless/canonical form}
\rightarrow
\text{heavy mathematics}
\rightarrow
\text{residual ledger}
}
$$

That matters for the formalization program, the solvers, the geometric machinery, and especially the Lagrangians.

For the heavy math generally, I would now make a distinction between two layers.

The first is the universal representational layer:

$$
w=ct,\qquad
X^i=\frac{x^i}{\ell_*},\qquad
W=\frac{w}{\ell_*},
$$

where \(\ell_*\) is extracted from the system rather than chosen cosmetically.

Then all derivatives become predictable:

$$
\partial_t=c\,\partial_w
=\frac{c}{\ell_*}\partial_W,
$$

$$
\nabla_x=\frac{1}{\ell_*}\nabla_X,
$$

$$
\partial_t^n
=
\left(\frac{c}{\ell_*}\right)^n
\partial_W^n.
$$

After that, the second layer is the actual mathematics: differential geometry, variational mechanics, topology, braid/holonomy machinery, Clifford algebra, PDE analysis, bifurcation analysis, numerical solution, whatever the problem actually requires.

That separation is valuable because it tells us which mathematical complexity is intrinsic to the model and which was just inherited from representation.

And yes: **we can automate a very large fraction of the conversion.**

I think we should.

The converter can operate on a symbolic representation of an equation and do, in order:

$$
t\mapsto w/c,
$$

$$
\frac{d}{dt}\mapsto c\frac{d}{dw},
$$

$$
\frac{d^n}{dt^n}\mapsto c^n\frac{d^n}{dw^n},
$$

then dimensional analysis of the resulting coefficients, extraction of candidate characteristic lengths, and finally

$$
x^\mu=\ell_*X^\mu.
$$

It can then output four things automatically:

1. the original equation;
2. the \(w\)-normalized equation;
3. a dimensionless canonical form;
4. the residuals that survived.

For example, feed it

$$
\ddot x+\omega^2x=0.
$$

It obtains

$$
x_{ww}+\frac{\omega^2}{c^2}x=0,
$$

recognizes

$$
\ell_\omega=\frac{c}{\omega},
$$

sets

$$
W=\frac{w}{\ell_\omega},
$$

and produces

$$
x_{WW}+x=0.
$$

Residual ledger: harmonic recurrence; no remaining dimensional parameter.

Feed it diffusion:

$$
T_t=D\nabla^2T,
$$

and it gets

$$
T_w=\frac Dc\nabla^2T,
$$

extracts

$$
\ell_D=\frac Dc,
$$

but also reports

$$
\boxed{\text{first temporal derivative versus second spatial derivative}}
$$

as a surviving operator anisotropy.

That is exactly the sort of thing software is better at doing consistently than we are manually.

The really interesting part is that we can apply the same machinery to our Lagrangians.

There is one technical correction worth making explicit because it matters here. If

$$
S=\int L(q,\dot q,t)\,dt
$$

and

$$
w=ct,
$$

then

$$
dt=\frac{dw}{c},
\qquad
\dot q=c\,q_w.
$$

Therefore

$$
S
=
\int
\frac1c
L\!\left(q,cq_w,\frac wc\right)
dw.
$$

So the properly transformed \(w\)-Lagrangian is

$$
\boxed{
L_w(q,q_w,w)
=
\frac1c
L\!\left(q,cq_w,\frac wc\right).
}
$$

That overall \(1/c\) does not alter the Euler--Lagrange trajectories when \(c\) is constant, which is why it is easy to overlook. But it absolutely matters if we want the action, canonical momentum, Hamiltonian, units, and cross-sector comparison to remain exact.

The Euler--Lagrange equations retain their form:

$$
\frac{d}{dw}
\frac{\partial L_w}{\partial q_w}
-
\frac{\partial L_w}{\partial q}
=0.
$$

And something rather nice happens to the canonical momentum:

$$
p_w
=
\frac{\partial L_w}{\partial q_w}
=
\frac{\partial L}{\partial\dot q}
=
p.
$$

So the momentum is unchanged by this particular reparameterization.

Meanwhile,

$$
H_w
=
p q_w-L_w
=
\frac1c(p\dot q-L)
=
\frac{H}{c}.
$$

Thus energy naturally turns into energy-per-\(c\), i.e. momentum dimensionality, just as one would expect when temporal evolution has been parameterized by a length.

That alone is worth putting in the paper.

For field theory the analogous statement is

$$
S
=
\int dt\,d^3x\,\mathcal L
=
\int dw\,d^3x\,
\frac{\mathcal L}{c},
$$

before any subsequent spatial normalization.

Then introduce a characteristic length

$$
x^\mu=\ell_*X^\mu.
$$

The four-volume measure becomes

$$
d^4x=\ell_*^4\,d^4X
$$

when all four coordinates are length-valued, and derivatives acquire factors of \(1/\ell_*\). This is where a complicated Lagrangian starts revealing which coefficients are genuinely independent dimensionless numbers.

And that brings us directly to the old and new H(s)H Lagrangians.

I would **not discard or rewrite either one yet**.

I would run both through the same normalization audit.

That gives us a genuinely useful comparison:

$$
\boxed{
L_{\rm old}
\longrightarrow
\widehat L_{\rm old}
}
$$

and

$$
\boxed{
L_{\rm new}
\longrightarrow
\widehat L_{\rm new}.
}
$$

Then compare the residual mathematical content rather than comparing their raw notation.

That could tell us that two terms which looked conceptually different were actually the same normalized invariant. Conversely, two terms we had been treating as analogous might remain inequivalent after everything removable is stripped away. That is exactly what we want to know.

For the newer finite-core/worldtube formulation, this gets especially interesting.

Suppose a curve or tube centerline has characteristic size \(\ell_*\), and write arc length as

$$
s=\ell_*\,\sigma.
$$

Then curvature scales as

$$
\kappa=\frac{\hat\kappa}{\ell_*},
$$

torsion as

$$
\tau=\frac{\hat\tau}{\ell_*},
$$

and derivatives of curvature scale as

$$
\frac{d\kappa}{ds}
=
\frac{1}{\ell_*^2}
\frac{d\hat\kappa}{d\sigma}.
$$

So a schematic geometric functional such as

$$
S
=
\int ds
\left[
A
+
B\kappa^2
+
C\tau^2
+
D\left(\frac{d\kappa}{ds}\right)^2
+\cdots
\right]
$$

becomes

$$
S
=
\int d\sigma
\left[
A\ell_*
+
\frac{B}{\ell_*}\hat\kappa^2
+
\frac{C}{\ell_*}\hat\tau^2
+
\frac{D}{\ell_*^3}
\left(\frac{d\hat\kappa}{d\sigma}\right)^2
+\cdots
\right].
$$

Then choose one natural action/energy scale and factor it out. What remains are dimensionless ratios such as

$$
\frac{B}{A\ell_*^2},
\qquad
\frac{C}{A\ell_*^2},
\qquad
\frac{D}{A\ell_*^4},
$$

or whatever the actual dimensional structure of our terms yields.

Those ratios—not \(A,B,C,D\) in their raw form—are much closer to the actual mathematical degrees of freedom of the theory.

For the recursive/nested hyperhelix Lagrangian, the same idea becomes even more important. Each scale order \(N\) can be described relative to its own inherited characteristic length,

$$
\ell_N,
$$

while the relations between levels are encoded by dimensionless ratios

$$
\rho_N=\frac{\ell_{N+1}}{\ell_N}.
$$

Suddenly “recursive scaling” ceases to mean carrying enormous or tiny dimensional numbers through the equations. It becomes a hierarchy of order-one geometries plus scale ratios.

Something like

$$
H_N(\lambda)
=
\ell_N\,
\widehat H_N(\lambda/\ell_N)
$$

could become the standard representation.

Then the genuinely interesting quantities are things like

$$
\frac{\ell_{N+1}}{\ell_N},
\quad
\hat\kappa_N,
\quad
\hat\tau_N,
\quad
\text{winding numbers},
\quad
\text{holonomies},
\quad
\text{chirality},
\quad
\text{bifurcation thresholds},
$$

rather than arbitrary absolute coordinate magnitudes.

That ties directly back to the Whirlygig, Spheres, and Graticule. They can all operate on the same normalized objects produced by the Lagrangian.

So I think the heavy-math roadmap actually becomes cleaner:

$$
\boxed{1.\ \text{Build the normalization engine}}
$$

$$
\boxed{2.\ \text{Normalize the old Lagrangian exactly}}
$$

$$
\boxed{3.\ \text{Normalize the new finite-core/worldtube Lagrangian exactly}}
$$

$$
\boxed{4.\ \text{Compare their dimensionless residual structures}}
$$

$$
\boxed{5.\ \text{Feed the canonical geometry into Whirlygig/Spheres/Graticule}}
$$

$$
\boxed{6.\ \text{formalize only the surviving kernel}}
$$

That last point is important. We had been contemplating a fairly large formalization effort—typed objects, admissibility, dynamics, readout, perhaps Lean, numerical solvers, etc.

I would now **delay formalizing arbitrary dimensional baggage**.

First normalize.

Then formalize the invariant/residual kernel.

That could substantially reduce the number of primitive constants, scale conventions, duplicated equations, and representation-specific cases that the formal system has to carry.

And there is one potentially major payoff: if the old SAT Lagrangians and the newer H(s)H Lagrangians normalize to substantially the same dimensionless core, we have a mathematically meaningful way of saying that the later formulation is a refinement of the earlier one rather than merely another notation.

If they do not, that is equally valuable. The residual mismatch tells us exactly where the theory actually changed.

So yes: I think this should now come **before** a lot of the heavy math we were planning—not because it replaces that math, but because it can tell us which heavy math is genuinely necessary.


It changes the heavy-math plan in a useful way: it gives us a common front end for almost all of it. It does not replace the heavy math; it tells us what coordinate/scale conventions to use before we start doing the heavy math, so we stop carrying avoidable asymmetries and arbitrary units through every derivation.

The rough architecture becomes

$$
\boxed{
\text{original formulation}
\rightarrow
\text{universal normalization}
\rightarrow
\text{natural system scale}
\rightarrow
\text{dimensionless/canonical form}
\rightarrow
\text{heavy mathematics}
\rightarrow
\text{residual ledger}
}
$$

$$

$$
\boxed{5.\ \text{Feed the canonical geometry into Whirlygig/Spheres/Graticule}}
$$

$$
\boxed{6.\ \text{formalize only the surviving kernel}}
$$

That last point is important. We had been contemplating a fairly large formalization effort—typed objects, admissibility, dynamics, readout, perhaps Lean, numerical solvers, etc.

I would now **delay formalizing arbitrary dimensional baggage**.

First normalize.

Then formalize the invariant/residual kernel.

That could substantially reduce the number of primitive constants, scale conventions, duplicated equations, and representation-specific cases that the formal system has to carry.

And there is one potentially major payoff: if the old SAT Lagrangians and the newer H(s)H Lagrangians normalize to substantially the same dimensionless core, we have a mathematically meaningful way of saying that the later formulation is a refinement of the earlier one rather than merely another notation.

If they do not, that is equally valuable. The residual mismatch tells us exactly where the theory actually changed.

So yes: I think this should now come **before** a lot of the heavy math we were planning—not because it replaces that math, but because it can tell us which heavy math is genuinely necessary.


It’s relativityvision—the dynamics only universe—the time residual fringe 

Yes. That phrase actually snaps several pieces together.

**Relativityvision** could be the viewing mode: take a system, strip away removable representational asymmetries, normalize its characteristic scales, and then look only at the relations that remain invariant or anisotropic under admissible reorientation.

The **dynamics-only universe** is what you get if you refuse to privilege static labels and instead describe everything by transformation, propagation, curvature, coupling, recurrence, deformation, and relative change. Not an ontology—just the most aggressively relational representation we can construct.

And the **time residual fringe** is the particularly interesting part. If we make the four-coordinate description as symmetric as the mathematics permits, then whatever still distinguishes the \(w\)-direction is no longer being supplied merely by seconds-versus-metres notation. It is the residual left after the easy asymmetry has been removed.

So schematically:

$$
\text{ordinary representation}
\;\xrightarrow{\;w=ct\;}
\text{common dimensional frame}
\;\xrightarrow{\;\text{scale normalization}\;}
\text{order-one system}
$$

$$
\xrightarrow{\;\text{lossless reorientation/symmetrization}\;}
\boxed{\text{residual anisotropy}}
$$

and within that residual,

$$
\boxed{\text{whatever persistently singles out the temporal direction}}
$$

is the **time fringe**.

That is much more interesting than starting by declaring a special time axis. We deliberately try to erase its specialness. Then we ask what we cannot erase.

And “fringe” is a surprisingly apt word because it need not be one giant categorical difference. It could be a small structured remainder distributed across several things:

$$
\text{signature},
\quad
\text{operator order},
\quad
\text{causal orientation},
\quad
\text{propagation direction},
\quad
\text{boundary conditions},
\quad
\text{irreversibility},
\quad
\text{phase/holonomy},
\quad
\text{expansion disequilibrium}.
$$

Some may turn out to be mutually reducible. Some may be independent. The audit tells us.

This also gives the Whirlygig a wonderfully simple mission:

$$
\boxed{\text{Try to rotate time away.}}
$$

Not literally remove time. Rotate/reparameterize/reorient the representation as far as exact recoverability allows.

Then:

**Whirlygig:** tries to remove directional privilege.

**Graticule:** maps the residual fringe.

**Spheres:** tests how that fringe behaves dynamically under deformation and bifurcation.

**Relativityvision/UI:** lets us see the normalized system, its transformations, and the residual simultaneously.

And the Lagrangian question becomes sharper too. We can ask of every term:

$$
\text{Does this term survive leveling?}
$$

If not, it was partly representational baggage. If yes, determine exactly what invariant or anisotropy it encodes.

That may be the best description yet of what our heavy-math reconstruction should actually be doing: **derive the dynamics of the residual fringe after maximal lawful symmetrization.**

Not “find an ontology underneath physics.” Not even “unify all equations.”

More like:

$$
\boxed{
\text{Level everything we legitimately can,
then mathematically characterize the grain that remains.}
}
$$

And if time really is the stubborn dominant grain across otherwise very different sectors, *that* would be a result rather than an assumption.


seconds-versus-metres notation

Although we should always be using meters and meters. Doesn’t matter which direction.

Exactly. That’s an important correction.

The baseline should not be “seconds versus metres.” It should be:

$$
\boxed{\text{metres versus metres}}
$$

from the outset.

So the temporal coordinate is already length-valued,

$$
w=ct,
$$

and every coordinate is expressed in the same dimensional currency. Then the question is not “what happens if we convert time into space-like units?” The conversion is just bookkeeping.

The real question is:

$$
\boxed{
\text{Given four length-valued coordinates, what asymmetry survives among them?}
}
$$

That is much cleaner.

And yes, the direction does not matter. We should not privilege a particular axis name more than the equations force us to. We can label the coordinates

$$
X^A,\qquad A=1,2,3,4
$$

with

$$
[X^A]=\mathrm{m}
$$

for all \(A\), and only later identify whichever direction carries the residual structure we conventionally call temporal.

So a stronger version of the method is:

$$
\text{equal dimensional footing}
\rightarrow
\text{normalize scale}
\rightarrow
\text{reorient freely within the admissible transformation class}
\rightarrow
\text{find the residual anisotropy}.
$$

That matters because if one axis is initially written in seconds and the others in metres, we have already baked an asymmetry into the representation before asking whether one exists.

Better to remove that immediately.

So I’d revise the language in the paper from “time-to-length normalization” toward something like:

> **common length-coordinate normalization**

or:

> **four-coordinate length normalization**

And instead of saying \(w=ct\) “makes time spatial,” say:

> \(w=ct\) places temporal displacement and spatial displacement in the same dimensional representation.

That is much closer to what we are actually doing.

Then “time residual fringe” becomes even sharper: it is the structure that still distinguishes one direction after **all four axes already have the same units and comparable scale**.

### **General Recurrent Normalization**
*   **Time-coordinate expressed in units of length:**
    `w = k t`
*   **Starting velocity:**
    `v = dx/dt`
*   **Normalized velocity:**
    `v/k = dx/dw`
*   **Equal-axis trajectory (where \\(k = c\\) and \\(v = c\\)):**
    `dx = dw` or `dx/dw = 1`

---

### **Move 1 — Velocity / Light**
*   **Starting equation:**
    `v = dx/dt`
*   **Normalization variable:**
    `w = kt`
*   **Normalized equation:**
    `v/k = dx/dw`
*   **Symmetrized light trajectory (where \\(v = c\\) and \\(k = c\\)):**
    `dx/dw = 1`

---

### **Move 2 — Wave Equation**
*   **Starting equation:**
    `∂²ψ/∂t² = u² ∇²ψ`
*   **Normalized wave equation (with \\(w = kt\\)):**
    `∂²ψ/∂w² = (u/k)² ∇²ψ`
*   **Symmetrized vacuum electromagnetic wave equation (where \\(u = c\\) and \\(k = c\\)):**
    `∂²ψ/∂w² = ∇²ψ`

---

### **Move 3 — Newtonian Acceleration and Force**
*   **Starting equations:**
    `a = d²x/dt²`
    `F = ma`
*   **Normalized equations (with \\(w = ct\\)):**
    `a = c² d²x/dw²`
    `F = mc² d²x/dw²`
*   **Acceleration expressed as curvature:**
    `d²x/dw² = a/c²`
*   **Force expressed as curvature-like tension:**
    `F = (mc²)(geometric curvature-like factor)`
*   **Integrated Newtonian kinetic energy (remains unchanged):**
    `K = 1/2 mv²`

---

### **Move 4 — Invariant Spacetime Relation**
*   **Minkowski invariant interval (with \\(w = ct\\)):**
    `ds² = dw² - dx²`
*   **Normalized coordinate displacement relative to coordinate slope (\\(\beta = dx/dw = v/c\\)):**
    `ds = dw sqrt(1 - β²)`
*   **Lorentz correction factor:**
    `γ = 1/sqrt(1 - β²)`
*   **Relativistic momentum:**
    `p = γmv`
*   **Relativistic energy:**
    `E = γmc²`
*   **Relativistic energy-momentum invariant:**
    `E² - p²c² = m²c⁴`

---

### **Move 5 — Diffusion / Heat**
*   **Starting equation:**
    `∂T/∂t = D ∇²T`
*   **Normalized diffusion equation (with \\(w = ct\\)):**
    `∂T/∂w = (D/c) ∇²T`
*   **Emergent characteristic length scale:**
    `l_D = D/c`

---

### **Move 6 — Free Schrödinger Equation**
*   **Starting equation:**
    `iħ ∂ψ/∂t = -(ħ²/2m) ∇²ψ`
*   **Spacetime-normalized wave equation (with \\(w = ct\\)):**
    `i ∂ψ/∂w = -(ħ/2mc) ∇²ψ`
*   **Reduced Compton wavelength scale:**
    `λbar_C = ħ/(mc)`
*   **Geometrized free-particle Schrödinger equation:**
    `i ∂ψ/∂w = -(λbar_C/2) ∇²ψ`

---

### **Move 7 — Hydrogen Schrödinger Equation**
*   **Starting equation:**
    `iħ ∂ψ/∂t = -(ħ²/2m_e) ∇²ψ - [e²/(4π ε0 r)] ψ`
*   **Spacetime-normalized equation (with \\(w = ct\\), \\(\lambdabar_C = ħ/(m_e c)\\), and \\(\alpha = e²/(4π ε0 ħ c)\\)):**
    `i ∂ψ/∂w = -(λbar_C/2) ∇²ψ - (α/r) ψ`
*   **Dimensionless coordinate scaling (where \\(\rho = r/\lambdabar_C\\)):**
    `i ∂ψ/∂τ = -(1/2) ∇²_ρ ψ - (α/ρ) ψ`

---

### **Summary of Normalized Geometric Scales**
The following transformations convert sector-specific coefficients into lengths, inverse lengths, or dimensionless ratios:
*   `ω  ->  ω/c` (inverse length)
*   `D  ->  D/c` (length)
*   `ħ/m  ->  ħ/(mc)` (Compton length)
*   `GM  ->  GM/c²` (gravitational length)
*   `qQ/(4π ε0 m)  ->  qQ/(4π ε0 mc²)` (electrostatic interaction length)

WORKING NOTE — DIMENSIONAL NORMALIZATION / REPRESENTATIONAL STRESS TEST

WHAT WE ARE DOING

We are testing whether familiar physical equations can be rewritten in a more dimensionally symmetric form by treating time as a coordinate to be numerically normalized against space, while preserving all information exactly.

The goal is not to “improve” standard physics, nor to claim that the rewritten geometry is physically fundamental. The goal is to ask:

1. Which apparent asymmetries are merely representational?
2. Which asymmetries survive every admissible reorientation/rescaling?
3. Which different physical systems reduce to the same normalized mathematical structure?
4. Where does finite scale, recurrence, curvature, crowding, nonlinearity, or some other constraint force the normalized representation to depart from perfect symmetry?
5. Can those departures be represented by a small, regular set of geometric quantities?

This is being treated as a representational audit, not an ontological claim.


CORE ASSUMPTION / METHOD

We begin as conservatively as possible:

- Do not grant time extra properties unless they are geometrically or empirically required.
- Begin by treating the dimensions as equivalently as possible.
- Introduce only measured dimensional differences.
- Use invertible transformations only: no information may be discarded.
- Prefer the simplest transformation first.
- If one axis is the only asymmetry, first test whether one common reorientation/normalization handles it.
- If multiple different rotations or representations preserve the same information, record that degeneracy as information about the representation.
- If no simple transformation works, do not force one.
- After normalization, identify the irreducible residue that cannot be transformed away.
- Apply the same operation across increasingly unrelated equations without retuning it equation by equation.

The recurring normalization has been

    w = k t

where w is a time-coordinate expressed in units of length.

Rather than initially assuming k = c, we asked what k does.

For velocity,

    v = dx/dt

becomes

    v/k = dx/dw.

Thus v/k is simply a dimensionless orientation/slope in the normalized x-w plane.

For a c-propagating trajectory, k = c uniquely gives

    dx = dw,

i.e. a 45-degree equal-axis trajectory.

This is the familiar ct normalization of relativity, reached by asking for numerical dimensional commensurability and maximal axis symmetry.


MOVE 1 — VELOCITY / LIGHT

Starting equation:

    v = dx/dt

Normalize:

    w = kt

giving

    v/k = dx/dw.

For v = c:

    k = c  ->  dx/dw = 1.

Result:

    c converts time intervals into the spatial units for which a light trajectory has equal coordinate increments.

Meaning:

This reproduces standard relativistic spacetime normalization. It is a calibration result, not new physics.


MOVE 2 — WAVE EQUATION

Starting equation:

    ∂²ψ/∂t² = u² ∇²ψ.

Normalize with w = kt:

    ∂²ψ/∂w² = (u/k)² ∇²ψ.

For electromagnetic waves, u = c.

Setting k = c gives

    ∂²ψ/∂w² = ∇²ψ.

The spatial and fourth-coordinate second derivatives now have equal coefficients.

Result:

The same k = c that symmetrizes the light trajectory also symmetrizes the vacuum electromagnetic wave equation.

Meaning:

Again, this is expected from relativity and electromagnetism and therefore functions as a calibration check.


MOVE 3 — NEWTONIAN ACCELERATION AND FORCE

Starting equations:

    a = d²x/dt²
    F = ma.

With w = ct:

    a = c² d²x/dw²

and therefore

    F = mc² d²x/dw².

So acceleration becomes a curvature-like quantity:

    d²x/dw² = a/c².

Force becomes

    F = (mc²)(geometric curvature-like factor).

Integrating the normalized Newtonian force law still produces

    K = 1/2 mv².

Result:

Normalization alone does NOT manufacture relativity.

It is an exact rewrite of Newtonian mechanics.

Meaning:

This is important because it prevents the procedure from becoming tautological. Equalizing the axes is not sufficient to generate relativistic corrections.


MOVE 4 — REQUIRE AN INVARIANT SPACETIME RELATION

Using normalized w = ct, impose the standard Minkowski invariant:

    ds² = dw² - dx².

For

    β = dx/dw = v/c,

this gives

    ds = dw sqrt(1 - β²)

and therefore

    γ = 1/sqrt(1 - β²).

From the normalized four-direction follow

    p = γmv

and

    E = γmc²,

with

    E² - p²c² = m²c⁴.

Result:

The relativistic correction emerges only after adding the invariant spacetime condition.

Meaning:

We have reconstructed special relativity by a somewhat different conceptual route:

    dimensional normalization
    ->
    equal-axis representation
    ->
    information-preserving invariant reorientation
    ->
    Lorentz/relativistic structure.

This is not historically new physics. It is a useful methodological reconstruction.


MOVE 5 — DIFFUSION / HEAT

Starting equation:

    ∂T/∂t = D ∇²T.

With w = ct:

    ∂T/∂w = (D/c) ∇²T.

Unlike the wave equation, the temporal derivative remains first order while the spatial derivative is second order.

The diffusivity becomes a length:

    l_D = D/c.

Result:

The same normalization does NOT make diffusion look like a relativistic wave equation.

Instead it converts its dimensional transport coefficient into a characteristic length.

Meaning:

Different classes of equations retain different structural asymmetries.

This is useful: the method distinguishes systems rather than forcing them all into one form.


MOVE 6 — FREE SCHRÖDINGER EQUATION

Starting equation:

    iħ ∂ψ/∂t = -(ħ²/2m) ∇²ψ.

With w = ct:

    i ∂ψ/∂w = -(ħ/2mc) ∇²ψ.

Define the reduced Compton wavelength

    λbar_C = ħ/(mc).

Then

    i ∂ψ/∂w = -(λbar_C/2) ∇²ψ.

Result:

After time-space normalization, the free-particle coefficient becomes a geometric length scale: one-half the reduced Compton wavelength.

Meaning:

The normalization translates quantum temporal evolution into a representation containing an intrinsic particle length scale.


MOVE 7 — HYDROGEN SCHRÖDINGER EQUATION

Starting equation:

    iħ ∂ψ/∂t
    =
    -(ħ²/2m_e) ∇²ψ
    -
    [e²/(4π ε0 r)] ψ.

With w = ct:

    i ∂ψ/∂w
    =
    -(λbar_C/2) ∇²ψ
    -
    (α/r) ψ,

where

    λbar_C = ħ/(m_e c)

and

    α = e²/(4π ε0 ħ c).

Now scale distance by λbar_C:

    ρ = r/λbar_C.

The equation becomes dimensionless:

    i ∂ψ/∂τ
    =
    -(1/2) ∇²_ρ ψ
    -
    (α/ρ) ψ.

Result:

After absorbing c and the electron Compton scale into the coordinate system, the electromagnetic coupling remains essentially as the dimensionless number α.

Also:

    a0 = λbar_C / α
    r_e = α λbar_C.

Thus

    r_e : λbar_C : a0

forms a simple scale chain separated by factors of α.

Meaning:

α can be represented as a scale ratio:

    α = λbar_C / a0.

This does NOT explain why α has its measured value. It shows how α functions geometrically in this representation.


MOVE 8 — HYDROGEN WINDING / HELIX INTERPRETATION

For the Bohr ground state:

    m_e v a0 = ħ

and therefore

    λ_dB = 2π a0.

Also

    λ_C = 2π λbar_C.

Therefore

    α
    =
    λ_C / λ_dB
    =
    λbar_C / a0
    =
    v/c.

Now represent one turn of a helix with

    radius R = a0
    pitch per turn P = λ_C.

Then

    tan θ
    =
    P/(2πR)
    =
    λ_C/(2πa0)
    =
    α.

So the candidate helix has

    tan θ = α.

Result:

The known hydrogen scales can be represented as a helix whose rise/run ratio is α.

Meaning:

This is NOT a derivation of α because the empirical scales were used to set the geometry.

For SAT/H(s)H, that is acceptable at this stage because the model is being used as a representational system. The question becomes whether the resulting geometry remains mutually compatible when the rest of physics is mapped onto it.


MOVE 9 — FINITE-CORE HELIX / CROWDING

For a helix

    r(φ) = (R cosφ, R sinφ, (P/2π)φ),

the distance between centerline points separated by Δφ is

    d²/R²
    =
    2(1 - cos Δφ)
    +
    a² Δφ²,

where

    a = P/(2πR).

For the hydrogen-scale assignment:

    a = α.

Giving the helix a finite core radius r_c produces a non-overlap limit determined primarily by neighboring turns.

At a = α:

    r_c,max / R ≈ π α ≈ 0.02293.

So the maximum circular core radius before adjacent-turn contact is about 2.29% of the helix radius.

The local bending limit is vastly looser; turn-to-turn packing dominates.

Result:

The finite-core representation develops a real geometric admissibility boundary.

Meaning:

This provides a way to define "crowding" without assuming physical elasticity.


MOVE 10 — BOUNCE / STRETCH AS GEOMETRIC RESPONSE

Define crowding by comparing the actual core radius with the contact limit.

Once the representation crosses the non-overlap boundary, some geometric parameter must change if overlap is forbidden.

Possible responses include:

    pitch increase
    radius change
    core compression
    cross-section deformation
    another admissible redistribution of the same information.

Near the α-scaled geometry, pitch adjustment is enormously more effective than radial expansion.

For small changes, approximately:

    fractional core growth ≈ fractional pitch growth.

If turn length is held fixed,

    (2πR)² + P² = constant,

then

    ΔR/R ≈ -α² ΔP/P.

Result:

A tightly wound α-helix possesses a strongly anisotropic geometric response even before any energetic or elastic law is introduced.

Meaning:

This is a possible representational precursor to what SAT/H(s)H informally calls bounce/stretch.

It is NOT yet a physical elasticity prediction.


MOVE 11 — HARMONIC OSCILLATOR ACROSS SECTORS

Classical oscillator:

    x¨ + ω²x = 0.

With w = ct:

    d²x/dw² + (ω/c)² x = 0.

Define

    l_ω = c/ω.

Then

    d²x/dw² + x/l_ω² = 0.

Using ξ = w/l_ω:

    x'' + x = 0.

The same occurs for an LC circuit:

    L Q¨ + Q/C = 0

which reduces under the same normalization to

    Q'' + Q = 0.

For the quantum harmonic oscillator, the normalization produces the characteristic spatial scale

    a_osc = sqrt(λbar_C l_ω)
          = sqrt(ħ/(mω)).

After scaling by a_osc and l_ω, the equation becomes

    i ∂ψ/∂τ
    =
    (1/2)(-∂²/∂ξ² + ξ²) ψ.

Result:

Mechanical, electrical, and quantum oscillators retain the same normalized recurrence grammar.

The quantum oscillator introduces an intermediate scale equal to the geometric mean of the Compton length and the temporal recurrence length.

Meaning:

The method is revealing common structure across physically different sectors without claiming that the sectors are physically identical.


MOVE 12 — COUPLED OSCILLATORS / FINITE RESOLUTION

For a discrete mass-spring chain,

    m x¨_n
    =
    K(x_{n+1} - 2x_n + x_{n-1}).

Its continuum limit is a wave equation.

The exact lattice dispersion is

    ω²
    =
    (4K/m) sin²(qa/2).

At long wavelength,

    sin(qa/2) ≈ qa/2,

and the system approaches the symmetric continuum wave equation.

As wavelength approaches the finite spacing a, the correction becomes

    sin(qa/2)/(qa/2).

Result:

A hidden finite recurrence scale produces a regular departure from the continuum symmetric representation.

Meaning:

This gives an explicit example of

    symmetric large-scale form
    +
    finite-resolution correction.

This is directly useful for SAT/H(s)H because it shows how "crowding" or finite structure can generate systematic deformation without inserting arbitrary correction terms.


MOVE 13 — NONLINEAR PENDULUM

Exact pendulum:

    θ¨ + (g/L) sinθ = 0.

After normalization and removal of the recurrence scale:

    θ'' + sinθ = 0.

The harmonic limit is

    θ'' + θ = 0.

The surviving finite-angle correction is therefore entirely

    sinθ - θ.

Result:

The symmetric harmonic structure remains as the small-amplitude core, while finite geometry produces a regular nonlinear departure.

Meaning:

Again, the normalized representation separates universal structure from a finite-geometry correction.


MOVE 14 — NEWTONIAN KEPLER GRAVITY

Starting equation:

    r¨ = -(GM/r³) r.

With w = ct:

    d²r/dw²
    =
    -(GM/c²) r/r³.

Define the gravitational length

    l_G = GM/c².

Then

    d²r/dw²
    =
    -l_G r/r³.

For a circular orbit:

    (v/c)² = l_G/r.

Result:

The Newtonian gravitational parameter becomes a length after dimensional normalization.

For the Sun:

    GM_sun/c² ≈ 1477 m.

Meaning:

This is half the Schwarzschild radius, but no GR was used to obtain it.

The appearance of GM/c² is therefore partly unavoidable once Newtonian gravity is expressed in coordinates where time is measured as a length.

This does NOT derive Schwarzschild geometry.


MOVE 15 — COULOMB VS KEPLER

Electrostatic attraction:

    m r¨
    =
    -[qQ/(4π ε0)] r/r³.

After w = ct:

    d²r/dw²
    =
    -l_E r/r³,

where

    l_E
    =
    |qQ|/(4π ε0 m c²).

This is structurally identical to normalized Newtonian gravity:

    l_G = GM/c².

For hydrogen:

    l_E
    =
    e²/(4π ε0 m_e c²)
    =
    r_e
    =
    α λbar_C.

For a circular orbit:

    (v/c)² = l_E/r.

At r = a0:

    (v/c)² = r_e/a0 = α²,

so

    v/c = α.

Result:

Normalized Newtonian gravity and normalized Coulomb attraction reduce to the same dimensionless inverse-square central-force geometry.

Their sector-specific content resides in the scale map, not the normalized equation.

Meaning:

This does NOT imply that gravity and electromagnetism are the same physical force.

It means that their inverse-square central-force structures belong to the same representational equivalence class after normalization.


WHAT WE HAVE FOUND SO FAR

A recurring pattern is emerging:

    ordinary dimensional equation
    ->
    normalize time using w = ct
    ->
    dimensional coefficients become lengths, inverse lengths, or dimensionless ratios
    ->
    remove the system-specific scale
    ->
    expose a simpler dimensionless geometric core
    ->
    identify the residual correction caused by coupling, finite scale, nonlinearity, curvature, or packing.

Examples:

    ω            ->  ω/c              inverse length
    D            ->  D/c              length
    ħ/m          ->  ħ/(mc)           Compton length
    GM           ->  GM/c²            gravitational length
    qQ/(4π ε0 m) ->  qQ/(4π ε0 mc²)  electrostatic interaction length

Different systems then use these scales differently:

    direct ratio
    square root
    geometric mean
    sine correction
    finite-spacing dispersion
    curvature/crowding limit
    nonlinear angular correction.

So far, the method has NOT collapsed all physics into one equation.

It has instead exposed families of equations that share normalized geometric structures while preserving their distinguishing information in scale ratios and correction terms.


WHAT THIS MEANS FOR STANDARD PHYSICS

At minimum:

- It is a systematic nondimensionalization/geometrization procedure.
- Much of it overlaps familiar techniques: ct coordinates, natural units, characteristic scales, nondimensionalization, similarity analysis, covariant formulation, principal-axis reasoning, normal modes, and scaling laws.
- Several recovered quantities are standard:
      reduced Compton wavelength
      classical electron radius
      Bohr radius
      GM/c²
      Lorentz γ
      normalized oscillator coordinates
      lattice dispersion.
- Nothing so far contradicts standard physics.
- Nothing so far constitutes a new derivation of measured constants.
- Nothing so far demonstrates that the geometric representation is ontologically fundamental.

The potentially interesting methodological question is whether ONE minimal normalization and representation grammar systematically compresses a very broad range of physics more uniformly than the usual sector-by-sector notation makes obvious.


WHAT THIS MEANS FOR SAT/H(s)H

For SAT/H(s)H, this is directly aligned with its intended role as a representational system.

It suggests a disciplined program:

1. Begin from measured standard physics.
2. Normalize dimensions as symmetrically as possible.
3. Preserve all information exactly.
4. Reorient to expose maximal orthogonality/symmetry.
5. Store displaced asymmetry in explicit recoverable quantities.
6. Identify common normalized structures across different sectors.
7. Introduce finite-core geometry only where required by the representation.
8. Measure crowding, curvature, closure, scale mismatch, and other admissibility limits.
9. Do not assume elasticity, forces, particles, fields, or ontology when geometry alone suffices.
10. Where the representation cannot fit all constraints simultaneously, quantify the minimum required deformation.
11. Compare that deformation with independent known physics only afterward.
12. Promote a geometric relation only if it works outside the equations or observations used to construct it.

In this framing, "bounce," "stretch," "pitch," "winding," etc. need not initially denote physical substances or mechanisms.

They can begin simply as names for measurable changes required to keep an information-preserving geometric representation admissible.


WHAT COULD BECOME INTERESTING

The stronger possibility would be that a single low-parameter geometric grammar repeatedly does all of the following without retuning:

- reproduces established covariant forms;
- maps unrelated sectors into the same normalized structures;
- converts familiar constants into simple scale/orientation relations;
- predicts which finite-scale corrections must appear;
- identifies otherwise unexplained dimensionless ratios;
- forces deformation magnitudes that correspond to independently measured quantities;
- predicts relationships in sectors not used to build the representation.

If that happens repeatedly, especially across independent sectors, the result would be more than a convenient visualization: it would be significant representational compression.

It would still not prove that the representation is "what reality is."

It would show that a surprisingly large fraction of established physics can be encoded by a common geometric grammar with fewer independent representational ingredients than are apparent in conventional formulations.


CURRENT BOTTOM LINE

So far:

    no contradiction,
    no new physics claim,
    no derivation of α,
    no proof of SAT/H(s)H.

But also:

    the normalization is behaving cleanly,
    standard results are recovered where they should be,
    unrelated sectors often collapse into shared dimensionless structures,
    finite geometry produces regular rather than arbitrary corrections,
    and the representation has not yet been forced to introduce ad hoc sector-specific machinery.

The correct next test is therefore not to seek confirmation.

It is to keep adding increasingly heterogeneous pieces of established physics, using the same normalization and bookkeeping rules, until the representation either:

    breaks,
    requires additional independent structure,
    develops a consistent hierarchy of geometric corrections,
    or continues compressing apparently distinct physics into common recoverable forms.

That failure/survival map is the experiment.


Yes. That is the stronger and more interesting version of the program.

Not:

> “Some equations can be made prettier by writing \(w=ct\).”

But:

> **Use the same information-preserving normalization and symmetry audit as a default probe across physics. Whatever can be normalized away is representational; whatever cannot be normalized away is candidate physical structure.**

The key is that failure is as informative as success.

For each equation, we would ask:

$$
\text{original law}
\rightarrow
\text{common normalization}
\rightarrow
\text{simplest canonical form}
\rightarrow
\boxed{\text{residual}}
$$

and then classify the residual rather than trying to eliminate it.

That residual might be:

$$
\text{a dimensionless coupling}
$$

$$
\text{a characteristic scale}
$$

$$
\text{different derivative order}
$$

$$
\text{a sign/signature difference}
$$

$$
\text{a characteristic speed }u/c
$$

$$
\text{nonlinearity}
$$

$$
\text{dispersion}
$$

$$
\text{finite resolution}
$$

$$
\text{dissipation}
$$

$$
\text{curvature}
$$

$$
\text{topology}
$$

or something we have not anticipated.

That is already what happened in our little sample.

The wave equation gave essentially complete coefficient symmetry after \(w=ct\).

Diffusion refused to do so because its temporal derivative is first order and its spatial derivative second order. That is useful information.

Schrödinger retained the Compton scale.

Hydrogen retained \(\alpha\).

The pendulum retained \(\sin\theta-\theta\).

The discrete oscillator chain retained its sinc-type finite-spacing correction.

Kepler and Coulomb collapsed into the same inverse-square canonical form but retained different scale maps.

So the output is not supposed to be “everything is the same.” It is something more precise:

$$
\boxed{
\text{identify exactly the extent to which systems are the same,
and exactly what prevents them from being more so.}
}
$$

That is very SAT-like in the methodological sense.

And it distinguishes this pretty sharply from Mukohyama–Uzan. They propose a mechanism and ask how broadly they can reproduce known Lorentzian physics. We are proposing a **general diagnostic applied to the known equations themselves**, including equations that are not relativistic field equations at all.

There is one important qualification to “all physics equations.” I would phrase it as **all physical laws for which the transformation is well-defined**, rather than claim every equation must literally contain \(t\). Pure algebraic constraints, state equations, topological conditions, conservation identities, etc. may enter at a different stage. But they can still be incorporated into the broader audit by transforming their variables into the same canonical representation and asking what invariants remain.

The methodological conjecture is therefore something like:

> **Physical equations should be subjected, wherever mathematically admissible, to a common lossless dimensional and symmetry normalization. Relations removed by that transformation are representational. Relations that survive it—or failures of the transformation itself—are retained as structured information about the system. Cross-sector recurrence of the same residual structures indicates shared mathematical organization; systematic incompatibilities delimit the representation or reveal additional required structure.**

And the really important part is the last clause:

$$
\boxed{\text{deviation is data.}}
$$

That makes the procedure falsifiable rather than a universal “geometry can describe anything” exercise.

If every equation needs arbitrary special handling, the proposed grammar has little explanatory value.

If a small fixed procedure works broadly and the deviations fall into a small number of recurring classes, that is representational compression.

If those residual classes then predict relationships we did not use to construct them, it becomes substantially more interesting.

Satobloc
HsH
